“This was the strongest internal conflict that I ever remember to have felt, and it was at the end of it that I perceived the first, but as yet very faint, symptoms of that sickness from which I shall not recover. Whether this be a token of mercy or no, my Father which is in heaven knows, but I know not.”

===Chinese===
「我当时该怎么办呢？」他事后对我讲，语气极为连贯、极为平静，「我同那位亲爱的伙计——虽说他不明白我意何指——订下过一桩交易：我该尽力补救我已闯下的祸，即在星期日当着民众挺身站出来，说明我是谁。诚然，他们是不会信我的。他们会看我的头发，见是黑的，而按理该是很浅的颜色。看到这里，他们便不会再往下看，多半会当场把我撕成碎片。就算官府验了尸，就算许多认识我的人——更不必说我种种体格尺寸和标记，二十年前便已登记在案——都认明那尸首正是我：当权的诸公，肯承认我就是太阳之子吗？才不呢。我如今身在太阳之宫，牵涉的利益太大，大到容不得我已在桑奇斯顿被撕成碎片——不管我被撕得多么千真万确；整桩事会被捂下去，至多落得一场异端，日后慢慢扑灭了事。

「话又说回来，『倘使这样、倘使那样』，与我何干？眼看一整个民族被一伙纯粹借他们渔利的人引入歧途，我难道不该挺身直言，无论结局如何？纵然我力所能及的微乎其微，难道不该把这微末之力使出来吗？那位好伙计的本能——如此直承天启、如此真确、如此健全——是怎么告诉他的？我自己的本能又是怎么回答的？任何一个正派人的良心又会怎么回答？谁还能有疑问呢？

「然而，理性不也一样存在吗？它不同本能一样，也是上帝所赐吗？记得年少时，我听过一首圣歌：『智慧从何处寻得呢？深渊说，不在我里面。』唱的人一遍遍反复咏问，我一遍遍黯然自语——『唉，何处，何处，到底何处？』待到那得胜的答案响起——『敬畏耶和华，便是智慧；远离恶，便是聪明』——我羞愧得缩进自己心里，恨不曾预先料到它。后来年纪渐长，当我试图拿这答案当作行路的灯光，却发现它只照出了另一个问题：『何谓敬畏耶和华？在这桩具体的事情里，何谓恶？』而我那套处置灵性两难的简便办法，原来不过是一例以玄解玄（*ignotum per ignotius*）。

「倘若撒但自己尚且时而化作光明的天使，光明的天使难道就不时而化作撒但的模样？倘若魔鬼并不如画上那样黑，上帝难道就始终那样白？还有一处经文不是说『敬畏耶和华是智慧的开端』么——说得好像这话并非此题的定论似的？人行恶以求善果，尚且不可；那么人行善而致恶果，难道就使得？纵然我挺身直言是善，我缄口忍住，岂不更善？

「就是这样一些无法无天、游移不定的念头，极其残忍地折磨着我，以致我做了一件多年未做之事——我祈求指引。『求你向我显明你的旨意，主啊，』我悲苦万分地呼喊，『显明之后，求你坚固我去遵行。』然而没有回答。本能把我往一头拽，理性把我往另一头拽。于是我拿定主意：且服从上帝赋我的理性，除非他同样赋我的本能把理性从我身上打出去。我至多只能想到这一步：主要怜悯谁，就怜悯谁；要叫谁刚硬，就叫谁刚硬。于是我再度祈求，愿我列于蒙他怜悯的人中间。

「这是我记得平生感受过的最激烈的一场内心交战；也正是在它收场之际，我初次察觉那场不治之症最初的、尚且极轻微的征兆。这究竟是不是怜悯的记号，我在天上的父知道，我却不知道。」

===Original===
From what my father afterwards told me, I do not think the above reflections had engrossed him for more than three or four minutes; the giddiness which had for some seconds compelled him to lay hold of the first thing he could catch at in order to avoid falling, passed away without leaving a trace behind it, and his path seemed to become comfortably clear before him. He settled it that the proper thing to do would be to buy some food, start back at once while his permit was still valid, help himself to the property which he had sold the Professors, leaving the Erewhonians to wrestle as they best might with the lot that it had pleased Heaven to send them.

This, however, was too heroic a course. He was tired, and wanted a night’s rest in a bed; he was hungry, and wanted a substantial meal; he was curious, moreover, to see the temple dedicated to himself, and hear Hanky’s sermon; there was also this further difficulty, he should have to take what he had sold the Professors without returning them their £4, 10s., for he could not do without his blanket, etc.; and even if he left a bag of nuggets made fast to the sucker, he must either place it where it could be seen so easily that it would very likely get stolen, or hide it so cleverly that the Professors would never find it. He therefore compromised by concluding that he would sup and sleep in Sunch’ston, get through the morrow as he best could without attracting attention, deepen the stain on his face and hair, and rely on the change so made in his appearance to prevent his being recognised at the dedication of the temple. He would do nothing to disillusion the people﻿—to do this would only be making bad worse. As soon as the service was over, he would set out towards the preserves, and, when it was well dark, make for the statues. He hoped that on such a great day the rangers might be many of them in Sunch’ston; if there were any about, he must trust the moonless night and his own quick eyes and ears to get him through the preserves safely.

The shops were by this time closed, but the keepers of a few stalls were trying by lamplight to sell the wares they had not yet got rid of. One of these was a bookstall, and, running his eye over some of the volumes, my father saw one entitled﻿—

===Chinese===
据父亲事后告诉我，上面这番思量，统共不过占了他三四分钟；此前有好几秒钟，一阵晕眩逼得他抓住随手够得着的头一样东西，才没有跌倒；此刻晕眩已消散得无影无踪，他面前的路也似乎变得舒坦而清楚。他拿定主意：正办是买些吃食，趁通行证还有效即刻动身回程，把卖给两位教授的东西径自取回，至于埃瑞洪人，且由他们同上天乐意降下的命运去各显神通地挣扎吧。

然而，这条路未免太豪迈了。他累了，想找张床睡一夜；他饿了，想饱餐一顿实实在在的饭；何况他还存着好奇，想看看那座奉献给他本人的神殿，听听汉基的布道。此外还有一层难处：要取回卖给教授们的东西，就得不退还他们的 4 英镑 10 先令，因为毯子等物他缺不得；就算留下一袋天然金块，拴牢在吸盘上，也要么摆在显眼之处，多半要被人偷走，要么藏得太过巧妙，教授们永远找它不着。于是他折中定计：且在桑奇斯顿吃晚饭、睡一宿，明天尽量不惹人注目地挨过去，把脸和头发上的染色加深，指望容貌一改，神殿落成典礼上便无人认出他来。他不打算做任何叫民众幻灭的事——那样只会把坏事弄得更坏。礼拜一完，他便动身往禁苑去；待天色黑透，再摸向雕像。他指望这样的大日子，巡山人们多半会齐集桑奇斯顿；万一还有留守的，他也只能仗着无月的夜色和自己伶俐的眼耳，平安穿过禁苑了。

此时铺子都已关门，只有几个摊主还在灯下兜售未脱手的货物。其中有个书摊，父亲把眼睛在几册书上一扫，见到一册题为——

===Original===
> *The Sayings of the Sunchild During His Stay in Erewhon*, to which is added a true account of his return to the palace of the sun with his Erewhonian bride. This is the only version authorised by the Presidents and Vice-Presidents of the Musical Banks; all other versions being imperfect and inaccurate.﻿—Bridgeford, XVIII, 150 pp. 8vo. Price 3s.

The reader will understand that I am giving the prices as nearly as I can in their English equivalents. Another title was﻿—

> *The Sacrament of Divorce: An Occasional Sermon* preached by Dr. Gurgoyle, President of the Musical Banks for the Province of Sunch’ston. 8vo, 16 pp. 6d.

Other titles ran﻿—

> *Counsels of Imperfection*. 8vo, 20 pp. 6d.
> *Hygiene; or, How to Diagnose Your Doctor*. 8vo, 10 pp. 3d.
> *The Physics of Vicarious Existence*, by Dr. Gurgoyle, President of the Musical Banks for the Province of Sunch’ston. 8vo, 20 pp. 6d.

===Chinese===
> *《太阳之子居埃瑞洪言行录》*，书末并附其偕埃瑞洪新娘返回太阳之宫始末信史。本版为音乐银行总长及副总长唯一核定之版本，其余诸本皆残缺讹误。——布里奇福德，XVIII 年，150 页，8 开本，定价 3 先令。

读者自会明白：这些价钱，我已是尽量折成英国等值报出的。

> *《离婚圣事：应时布道一篇》*，桑奇斯顿省音乐银行总长古戈伊尔博士（Dr. Gurgoyle）宣讲。8 开本，16 页，6 便士。

其余书名有——

> *《不完美箴言》*。8 开本，20 页，6 便士。
> *《卫生学；或，如何给你的医生下诊断》*。8 开本，10 页，3 便士。
> *《代人生存的物理学》*，桑奇斯顿省音乐银行总长古戈伊尔博士著。8 开本，20 页，6 便士。

===Original===
There were many other books whose titles would probably have attracted my father as much as those that I have given, but he was too tired and hungry to look at more. Finding that he could buy all the foregoing for 4s. 9d., he bought them and stuffed them into the valise that he had just bought. His purchases in all had now amounted to a little over £1, 10s. (silver), leaving him about £3 (silver), including the money for which he had sold the quails, to carry him on till Sunday afternoon. He intended to spend say £2 (silver), and keep the rest of the money in order to give it to the British Museum.

He now began to search for an inn, and walked about the less fashionable parts of the town till he found an unpretending tavern, which he thought would suit him. Here, on importunity, he was given a servant’s room at the top of the house, all others being engaged by visitors who had come for the dedication. He ordered a meal, of which he stood in great need, and having eaten it, he retired early for the night. But he smoked a pipe surreptitiously up the chimney before he got into bed.

Meanwhile other things were happening, of which, happily for his repose, he was still ignorant, and which he did not learn till a few days later. Not to depart from chronological order I will deal with them in my next chapter.

===Chinese===
此外还有许多书，书名想也会像上列这些一样吸引父亲，可惜他又累又饿，不能再看了。一问之下，上列各书共值 4 先令 9 便士，他便统统买下，塞进刚买的那只旅行提包。至此他购物总数已略超过 1 英镑 10 先令（银币）；把卖鹌鹑所得一并计入，手头还剩约 3 英镑（银币），足够撑到星期日下午。他打算花去约 2 英镑（银币），余下的钱留着，日后送给大英博物馆。

他这才开始寻客栈，在城里不那么时髦的地段转来转去，直到找到一家不事张扬的小客栈，觉得正合己用。在这里，经他再三恳求，店家才拨给他屋顶下的一间仆役房——其余客房早被赶来观礼的游客订一空了。他叫了一顿饭——他正饥肠辘辘——吃罢便早早安置。只是上床之前，他躲着人凑近烟囱，偷偷抽了一斗烟。

与此同时，别的一些事情正在发生；于他安眠大幸的是，他尚一无所知，直到几天之后才有所闻。为不乱编年次序，我且到下一章里再叙。

## XV The Temple Is Dedicated to My Father, and Certain Extracts Are Read from His Supposed Sayings / 十五、神殿落成，奉献于我父亲名下，所谓《言行录》若干段落当众宣读

===Original===
“It is enough to break one’s heart,” said Mr. Balmy when he had outstripped the procession, and my father was again beside him. “ ‘As well as,’ indeed! We know what that means. Wherever there is a factory there is a hotbed of unbelief. ‘As well as’! Why it is a defiance.”
“What, I wonder,” said my father innocently, “must the Sunchild’s feelings be, as he looks down on this procession. For there can be little doubt that he is doing so.”
“There can be no doubt at all,” replied Mr. Balmy, “that he is taking note of it, and of all else that is happening this day in Erewhon. Heaven grant that he be not so angered as to chastise the innocent as well as the guilty.”

===Chinese===
「真是让人心碎，」巴尔米先生赶过了那支游行队伍、我父亲又回到他身旁时，开口说道。「『并重』，亏他们写得出！这算什么意思，我们心里都明白。凡有工厂的地方，就有不信神的温床。『并重』！这分明是挑衅。」
「不知道，」我父亲一副无辜的天真模样，说道，「太阳之子俯瞰着这支队伍的时候，心里该是什么滋味呢。因为他此刻正俯瞰着，这差不多是没有疑问的。」
「那是丝毫也不容怀疑的，」巴尔米先生答道，「他正把这桩事看在眼里、记在心上，连同今日埃瑞洪发生的其余一切。但愿上天保佑，别叫他动那么大的怒，连无辜者也连同有罪的一并责罚。」

===Original===
“I doubt,” said my father, “his being so angry even with this procession, as you think he is.”
Here, fearing an outburst of indignation, he found an excuse for rapidly changing the conversation. Moreover he was angry with himself for playing upon this poor good creature. He had not done so of malice prepense; he had begun to deceive him, because he believed himself to be in danger if he spoke the truth; and though he knew the part to be an unworthy one, he could not escape from continuing to play it, if he was to discover things that he was not likely to discover otherwise.
Often, however, he had checked himself. It had been on the tip of his tongue to be illuminated with the words,

===Chinese===
「至于他对这支游行队伍是不是真如您想的那么动怒，」我父亲说，「我倒有些疑心。」
说到这里，他生怕对方义愤勃发，便寻了个由头，赶紧把话岔开。况且，他拿这个可怜的好人打趣，自己也着实生自己的气。他并非蓄意如此；当初着手欺瞒，只因觉得说了真话便有性命之虞；如今他虽明知这个角色不体面，却也脱不了身，只得接着演下去——因为有些事情，不走这一条路，他便再无别法探知。
不过他也不时克制住自己。有好几回，底下这样的话已经到了舌尖，险些借着灵启说了出来——

===Original===
> Sukoh and Sukop were two pretty men,
 They lay in bed till the clock struck ten,
and to follow it up with,
> Now with the drops of this most Yknarc time
 My love looks fresh,
in order to see how Mr. Balmy would interpret the assertion here made about the Professors, and what statement he would connect with his own Erewhonian name; but he had restrained himself.

===Chinese===
> 苏科和苏科普是两个漂亮人，
 一觉躺到时钟敲十点，
再顺势接上一句——
> 如今沾着这最伊克纳克（Yknarc）时辰的露水，
 我的爱人容色鲜妍，
——好看看巴尔米先生会怎样解读这几句里关于两位教授的断言，又会把哪一句话联系到他自己的埃瑞洪名字上头。可他终究忍住了。

===Original===
The more he saw, and the more he heard, the more shocked he was at the mischief he had done. See how he had unsettled the little mind this poor, dear, good gentleman had ever had, till he was now a mere slave to preconception. And how many more had he not in like manner brought to the verge of idiocy? How many again had he not made more corrupt than they were before, even though he had not deceived them﻿—as for example, Hanky and Panky. And the young? how could such a lie as that a chariot and four horses came down out of the clouds enter seriously into the life of anyone, without distorting his mental vision, if not ruining it?
And yet, the more he reflected, the more he also saw that he could do no good by saying who he was. Matters had gone so far that though he spoke with the tongues of men and angels he would not be listened to; and even if he were, it might easily prove that he had added harm to that which he had done already. No. As soon as he had heard Hanky’s sermon, he would begin to work his way back, and if the Professors had not yet removed their purchase, he would recover it; but he would pin a bag containing about five pounds worth of nuggets on to the tree in which they had hidden it, and, if possible, he would find some way of sending the rest to George.
He let Mr. Balmy continue talking, glad that this gentleman required little more than monosyllabic answers, and still more glad, in spite of some agitation, to see that they were now nearing Sunch’ston, towards which a great concourse of people was hurrying from Clearwater, and more distant towns on the main road. Many whole families were coming﻿—the fathers and mothers carrying the smaller children, and also their own shoes and stockings, which they would put on when nearing the town. Most of the pilgrims brought provisions with them. All wore European costumes, but only a few of them wore it reversed, and these were almost invariably of higher social status than the great body of the people, who were mainly peasants.

===Chinese===
他见得越多、听得越多，对自己闯下的祸害便越是心惊。瞧啊，这位可怜、亲爱、善良的先生，本来头脑就只有那么一点点，如今又被他搅得心神无主，沦为彻头彻尾的成见之奴。像这样被他推到愚痴边缘的，又何止一人？再有些人，他虽不曾欺瞒，却也被他弄得比先前更坏了——汉基和潘基便是一例。还有年轻人呢？「一辆四驾马车从云端降了下来」，这样的谎话一旦当真进入了谁的生活，怎能不叫他心目的视力歪斜，甚至把它毁掉？
然而他越是思量，也越是明白：道出自己是谁，已无济于事。事情闹到了这步田地，纵使他说出人和天使的方言，也无人肯听；即便有人肯听，弄到最后，多半还要在他已有的祸害之上再添一重。不成的。等他听完汉基的布道，他就设法往回走。两位教授买下的那批东西若还没有挪走，他便把它取回来；不过他要钉一只袋子在藏金的那棵树上，袋里装着约值五镑的金块；至于其余的，只要办得到，他总要设法送到乔治手里。
他由着巴尔米先生絮絮谈下去，暗自庆幸这位先生所要的不过是单音节的答话；而更庆幸的是——尽管心头还有几分忐忑——他们此刻已经望见桑奇斯顿了。从清水城，从大路上更远的城镇，大批的人群正络绎赶往那里。许多是阖家而来——做父亲的做母亲的，怀里抱着年幼的孩子，手里还拎着自家的鞋袜，预备临近城边时再穿上。朝香客多半自带干粮。人人都穿着欧式服装，只是把衣裳反穿的人寥寥无几，而这些人几乎无一例外比芸芸众生的地位要高，后者大都是庄稼人。

===Original===
When they reached the town, my father was relieved at finding that Mr. Balmy had friends on whom he wished to call before going to the temple. He asked my father to come with him, but my father said that he too had friends, and would leave him for the present, while hoping to meet him again later in the day. The two, therefore, shook hands with great effusion, and went their several ways. My father’s way took him first into a confectioner’s shop, where he bought a couple of Sunchild buns, which he put into his pocket, and refreshed himself with a bottle of Sunchild cordial and water. All shops except those dealing in refreshments were closed, and the town was gaily decorated with flags and flowers, often festooned into words or emblems proper for the occasion.
My father, it being now a quarter to eleven, made his way towards the temple, and his heart was clouded with care as he walked along. Not only was his heart clouded, but his brain also was oppressed, and he reeled so much on leaving the confectioner’s shop, that he had to catch hold of some railings till the faintness and giddiness left him. He knew the feeling to be the same as what he had felt on the Friday evening, but he had no idea of the cause, and as soon as the giddiness left him he thought there was nothing the matter with him.
Turning down a side street that led into the main square of the town, he found himself opposite the south end of the temple, with its two lofty towers that flanked the richly decorated main entrance. I will not attempt to describe the architecture, for my father could give me little information on this point. He only saw the south front for two or three minutes, and was not impressed by it, save in so far as it was richly ornamented﻿—evidently at great expense﻿—and very large. Even if he had had a longer look, I doubt whether I should have got more out of him, for he knew nothing of architecture, and I fear his test whether a building was good or bad, was whether it looked old and weather-beaten or no. No matter what a building was, if it was three or four hundred years old he liked it, whereas, if it was new, he would look to nothing but whether it kept the rain out. Indeed I have heard him say that the medieval sculpture on some of our great cathedrals often only pleases us because time and weather have set their seals upon it, and that if we could see it as it was when it left the mason’s hands, we should find it no better than much that is now turned out in the Euston Road.

===Chinese===
到了城里，我父亲松了口气——原来巴尔米先生有几位朋友，进神殿之前要先去拜望。他邀我父亲同去，我父亲却说自己也另有朋友，此刻只好暂且分手，仍指望当天晚些时候再与他相会。于是二人热络络地执手作别，各奔一方。我父亲一路先走进一家糖果点心铺，买了两只太阳之子圆甜面包揣进衣袋，又喝了一瓶兑水的太阳之子药酒提神。除了卖饮食的铺子，家家店门都已关闭；满城彩旗鲜花，装点得喜气洋洋，往往编缀成应景的字句或徽记。
这时已是十一点差一刻，我父亲便朝神殿走去，一路心头愁云密布。愁云密布的又何止心头，他的脑子也昏沉压抑；走出糖果点心铺时他晃晃悠悠，险些站不住，只得抓住一段栏杆，直到那阵虚弱眩晕过去。他知道这滋味同星期五傍晚所感受的一模一样，却全然不知病根何在；而眩晕一过，他便只道自己安然无恙了。
拐进一条通到城中大广场的侧街，他发觉自己正对着神殿的南端，两座高塔夹峙着装饰华丽的正门。建筑的形制我不打算描摹，因为父亲在这上头提供不出多少见闻。南立面他统共只看了两三分钟，并不觉得怎样——只看出它雕缋满眼，一望便知耗费不赀，而且极大，如此而已。即便他多看上一阵，我怕也问不出更多的名堂：他对建筑一窍不通，而一座房子究竟是好是坏，他的考验标准，我恐怕就是看它模样古老、饱经风雨与否。不论一座建筑是什么路数，只要有了三四百年屋龄，他便中意；若是新盖的，他便只问一样——漏不漏雨。的确，我听他说过：我们几座大教堂上的中世纪雕刻，之所以往往讨人喜欢，不过是因为时光和风雨在它上面盖下了印玺；倘若我们能看见它才出石匠之手时的本来面目，便会发觉它并不比如今尤斯顿路上大量炮制出来的东西高明多少。

===Original===
The ground plan here given will help the reader to understand the few following pages more easily.
*a.* Table with cashier’s seat on either side, and alms-box in front. The picture is exhibited on a scaffolding behind it.
*b.* The reliquary.
*c.* The President’s chair.
*d.* Pulpit and lectern.
*e. f. g. h.* Side doors.
*i.* Yram’s seat.
*k.* Seats of George and the Sunchild.
*o’* Pillars.

===Chinese===
这里附上一幅平面图，读者借助它，可以更容易读懂随后几页。
*a.* 圣桌，两侧各设出纳员座席，前面设奉献箱。圣像画陈列于桌后高架之上。
*b.* 圣物匣。
*c.* 总长的座椅。
*d.* 讲坛与读经台。
*e. f. g. h.* 侧门。
*i.* 雅拉姆的座席。
*k.* 乔治与太阳之子的座位。
*o’* 柱。

===Original===
*A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H*, blocks of seats.
*I.* Steps leading from the apse to the nave.
*K* and *L.* Towers.
*M.* Steps and main entrance.
*N.* Robing-room.

===Chinese===
*A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H*，成片座区。
*I.* 由后殿通向中殿的台阶。
*K* 与 *L.* 塔楼。
*M.* 台阶与正门。
*N.* 更衣室。

===Original===
The building was led up to by a flight of steps (M), and on entering it my father found it to consist of a spacious nave, with two aisles and an apse which was raised some three feet above the nave and aisles. There were no transepts. In the apse there was the table (a), with the two bowls of Musical Bank money mentioned on an earlier page, as also the alms-box in front of it.
At some little distance in front of the table stood the President’s chair (c), or I might almost call it throne. It was so placed that his back would be turned towards the table, which fact again shows that the table was not regarded as having any greater sanctity than the rest of the temple.
Behind the table, the picture already spoken of was raised aloft. There was no balloon; some clouds that hung about the lower part of the chariot served to conceal the fact that the painter was uncertain whether it ought to have wheels or no. The horses were without driver, and my father thought that someone ought to have had them in hand, for they were in far too excited a state to be left safely to themselves. They had hardly any harness, but what little there was was enriched with gold bosses. My mother was in Erewhonian costume, my father in European, but he wore his clothes reversed. Both he and my mother seemed to be bowing graciously to an unseen crowd beneath them, and in the distance, near the bottom of the picture, was a fairly accurate representation of the Sunch’ston new temple. High up, on the right hand, was a disc, raised and gilt, to represent the sun; on it, in low relief, there was an indication of a gorgeous palace, in which, no doubt, the sun was supposed to live; though how they made it all out my father could not conceive.

===Chinese===
一道台阶（M）把人引上殿去。一进殿门，我父亲便看出殿内是一所宽敞的中殿，带两条侧廊，尽头一座后殿，比中殿和侧廊高出约三英尺。殿内没有交叉袖厅。后殿里设着那张圣桌（a），桌上有前文提过的那两钵音乐银行钱币，桌前还放着那只奉献箱。
离圣桌不远的前方，置着总长的座椅（c）——或者几乎不妨称它宝座。座位安放得使他背朝圣桌；这一层又表明：圣桌并不被看作比殿内其余处所更具神圣性。
圣桌后面，前文说过的那幅画高高悬起。画上没有气球；马车下半部萦绕着几朵云，恰好遮掩了一件事：画师吃不准它究竟该不该有轮子。四匹马没有御者，我父亲心想总该有个人执辔才是，因为它们亢奋得太厉害，撒手不管断乎放心不下。马身上鞍辔几乎全无，仅有的那一点点还镶着金圆饰钉。我母亲穿埃瑞洪装束，我父亲穿欧式服装，只是衣裳反穿着。夫妇俩似乎正朝脚下看不见的人群蔼然俯身致意；远景里、靠近画幅下方，是对桑奇斯顿新神殿相当忠实的描摹。右上方高处悬着一轮凸起的鎏金圆盘，代表太阳；盘上以浅浮雕示意出一座富丽的宫殿——太阳想必就住在这里面——虽然这一切他们是如何考究出来的，我父亲百思不得其解。

===Original===
On the right of the table there was a reliquary (b) of glass, much adorned with gold, or more probably gilding, for gold was so scarce in Erewhon that gilding would be as expensive as a thin plate of gold would be in Europe: but there is no knowing. The reliquary was attached to a portable stand some five feet high, and inside it was the relic already referred to. The crowd was so great that my father could not get near enough to see what it contained, but I may say here, that when, two days later, circumstances compelled him to have a close look at it, he saw that it consisted of about a dozen fine coprolites, deposited by some antediluvian creature or creatures, which, whatever else they may have been, were certainly not horses.
In the apse there were a few cross benches (G and H) on either side, with an open space between them, which was partly occupied by the President’s seat already mentioned. Those on the right, as one looked towards the apse, were for the Managers and Cashiers of the Bank, while those on the left were for their wives and daughters.
In the centre of the nave, only a few feet in front of the steps leading to the apse, was a handsome pulpit and lectern (d). The pulpit was raised some feet above the ground, and was so roomy that the preacher could walk about in it. On either side of it there were cross benches with backs (E and F); those on the right were reserved for the Mayor, civic functionaries, and distinguished visitors, while those on the left were for their wives and daughters.
Benches with backs (A, B, C, D) were placed about halfway down both nave and aisles﻿—those in the nave being divided so as to allow a free passage between them. The rest of the temple was open space, about which people might walk at their will. There were side doors (e, j, and f, h) at the upper and lower end of each aisle. Over the main entrance was a gallery in which singers were placed.

===Chinese===
圣桌右首有一只玻璃圣物匣（b），满饰黄金——或者更可能是镀金，因为埃瑞洪的黄金奇缺，镀金的价钱抵得上欧洲打一面薄金板：不过这也难说。圣物匣安在一座约五英尺高的活动架上，匣中便是前文提到的那件圣物。人群拥挤不堪，我父亲凑不到近前看清内藏何物；不过我可以在此补一句：两天以后，情势所迫，他得以细看了一回，才看清那是约莫一打上好的粪化石，出自洪荒以前的某种生物——或者不止一种——之所遗；这生物不管是何等角色，断乎不是马。
后殿两侧各有几排横凳（G 与 H），中间空出一块，一部分被前述总长的座位占着。朝后殿望去，右边的横凳留给银行的各位管理人及出纳员，左边的留给他们的妻女。
中殿正中、离通后殿的台阶只有几英尺的地方，设着一座气派的讲坛连读经台（d）。讲坛高出地面几英尺，宽敞得布道人尽可在里面踱步。讲坛两侧是有靠背的横凳（E 与 F）；右边的留给市长、市政官员及莅临的贵宾，左边的留给他们的妻女。
带靠背的长凳（A、B、C、D）安置在中殿和两侧廊约居中处——中殿里的长凳分隔成行，行间留出通道。殿内其余地方都是空场，任人随意走动。每条侧廊的两头各有侧门（e、j 和 f、h）。正门上方是一座楼座，唱诗的人便安置在那里。

===Original===
As my father was worming his way among the crowd, which was now very dense, he was startled at finding himself tapped lightly on the shoulder, and turning round in alarm was confronted by the beaming face of George.
“How do you do, Professor Panky?” said the youth﻿—who had decided thus to address him. “What are you doing here among the common people? Why have you not taken your place in one of the seats reserved for our distinguished visitors? I am afraid they must be all full by this time, but I will see what I can do for you. Come with me.”
“Thank you,” said my father. His heart beat so fast that this was all he could say, and he followed meek as a lamb.

===Chinese===
我父亲正在人丛里蛇行——这时已拥挤不堪——忽觉肩头被人轻轻一拍，吃了一惊；慌忙回顾，迎面正撞上乔治的一张灿然的笑脸。
「潘基教授，您好啊？」年轻人说道——他已拿定主意这样称呼他。「您怎么混在老百姓堆里？您怎么不在贵宾专席上就座？只怕这时候早座无虚席了，不过我总想想办法。跟我来。」
「谢谢你。」我父亲说。他的心跳得太快，只说得出这一句，便绵羊一般乖乖跟了去。

===Original===
With some difficulty the two made their way to the right-hand corner seats of block C, for every seat in the reserved block was taken. The places which George wanted for my father and for himself were already occupied by two young men of about eighteen and nineteen, both of them well-grown, and of prepossessing appearance. My father saw by the truncheons they carried that they were special constables, but he took no notice of this, for there were many others scattered about the crowd. George whispered a few words to one of them, and to my father’s surprise they both gave up their seats, which appear on the plan as (k).
It afterwards transpired that these two young men were George’s brothers, who by his desire had taken the seats some hours ago, for it was here that George had determined to place himself and my father if he could find him. He chose these places because they would be near enough to let his mother (who was at i, in the middle of the front row of block E, to the left of the pulpit) see my father without being so near as to embarrass him; he could also see and be seen by Hanky, and hear every word of his sermon; but perhaps his chief reason had been the fact that they were not far from the side-door at the upper end of the right-hand aisle, while there was no barrier to interrupt rapid egress should this prove necessary.
It was now high time that they should sit down, which they accordingly did. George sat at the end of the bench, and thus had my father on his left. My father was rather uncomfortable at seeing the young men whom they had turned out, standing against a column close by, but George said that this was how it was to be, and there was nothing to be done but to submit. The young men seemed quite happy, which puzzled my father, who of course had no idea that their action was preconcerted.
Panky was in the first row of block F, so that my father could not see his face except sometimes when he turned round. He was sitting on the Mayor’s right hand, while Dr. Downie was on his left; he looked at my father once or twice in a puzzled way, as though he ought to have known him, but my father did not think he recognised him. Hanky was still with President Gurgoyle and others in the robing-room, N; Yram had already taken her seat: my father knew her in a moment, though he pretended not to do so when George pointed her out to him. Their eyes met for a second; Yram turned hers quickly away, and my father could not see a trace of recognition in her face. At no time during the whole ceremony did he catch her looking at him again.

===Chinese===
两人费了些周折，才挤到 C 区右角的座位，因为专席区早已座无虚席。乔治想给我父亲和自己占的位置上，坐着两个年轻人，年约十八九岁，都生得高大，仪容也讨喜。我父亲看见他们携带的警棍，知道是特别警员（special constables），但并未在意，因为人群里还散布着许多这样的。乔治对其中的一个咬了几句耳朵，不料两人竟都让出了座位——那座位在平面图上标作（k）。
后来才弄清楚，这两个年轻人原来是乔治的弟弟，奉他的吩咐几小时前便来占了座位：因为倘若找得到我父亲，乔治拿定主意要把他和自己安顿在这里。他挑中这两个位置，是因为离他母亲不远不近正合适（她坐在 i，即讲坛左边 E 区前排正中），既让她看得见父亲，又不至于近得叫他局促；他自己也望得见汉基，也叫汉基望得见他，布道的字字句句都听得真切；不过他最主要的理由，恐怕还在于这里离右廊上端的侧门不远，而一旦情势所需，中间又没有任何栏杆阻着，要迅速脱身是便当的。
这时也到了非坐下不可的时候，两人便落了座。乔治坐在凳头，我父亲便在他左手。被他们撵起来的那两个年轻人倚在近旁一根柱子上，我父亲瞧着颇为不安；乔治却说事情本来就该这样，除了顺从没有别的法子。两个年轻人看上去还挺快活，这叫我父亲纳闷——他当然想不到这一手是事先串通好了的。
潘基坐在 F 区第一排，我父亲望不见他的脸，只有他偶尔回头时才得一见。他坐在市长右手，唐尼博士坐在市长左手。他有一两回带着困惑的神情打量我父亲，仿佛自己本该认得此人；但我父亲断定他并没有认出来。汉基还在更衣室（N）里，同古戈伊尔总长等人在一起；雅拉姆早已入座：我父亲一眼便认出了她，只是当乔治把她指给他看时，他装作不识。两人目光相接了一瞬；雅拉姆倏地移开眼去，她脸上我父亲看不出一丝认出来的痕迹。整个典礼自始至终，他再没有撞见她朝自己望过一眼。

===Original===
“Why, you stupid man,” she said to him later on in the day with a quick, kindly smile, “I was looking at you all the time. As soon as the President or Hanky began to talk about you I knew you would stare at him, and then I could look. As soon as they left off talking about you I knew you would be looking at me, unless you went to sleep﻿—and as I did not know which you might be doing, I waited till they began to talk about you again.”
My father had hardly taken note of his surroundings when the choir began singing, accompanied by a few feeble flutes and lutes, or whatever the name of the instrument should be, but with no violins, for he knew nothing of the violin, and had not been able to teach the Erewhonians anything about it. The voices were all in unison, and the tune they sang was one which my father had taught Yram to sing; but he could not catch the words.
As soon as the singing began, a procession, headed by the venerable Dr. Gurgoyle, President of the Musical Banks of the province, began to issue from the robing-room, and move towards the middle of the apse. The President was sumptuously dressed, but he wore no mitre, nor anything to suggest an English or European Bishop. The Vice-President, Head Manager, Vice-Manager, and some Cashiers of the Bank, now ranged themselves on either side of him, and formed an impressive group as they stood, gorgeously arrayed, at the top of the steps leading from the apse to the nave. Here they waited till the singers left off singing.
When the litany, or hymn, or whatever it should be called, was over, the Head Manager left the President’s side and came down to the lectern in the nave, where he announced himself as about to read some passages from the *Sunchild’s Sayings*. Perhaps because it was the first day of the year according to their new calendar, the reading began with the first chapter, the whole of which was read. My father told me that he quite well remembered having said the last verse, which he still held as true; hardly a word of the rest was ever spoken by him, though he recognised his own influence in almost all of it. The reader paused, with good effect, for about five seconds between each paragraph, and read slowly and very clearly. The chapter was as follows:﻿—

===Chinese===
「嗐，你这个笨人，」当天晚些时候，她带着一个敏捷而和蔼的微笑对他说道，「我一直都在看你呀。总长或是汉基一开口讲到你，我就知道你必定盯着他们看，那时我便可以看你了。他们一不讲你，我就知道你要来看我了——除非你睡着了。我不知道你正做着哪一样，只好等他们再讲起你来。」
我父亲还没来得及看清四周，唱诗班便唱起来了，伴奏的是几支有气无力的笛子和琉特琴——或者随便那乐器叫什么名字吧——只是没有小提琴：因为他对小提琴一无所知，也就无从教给埃瑞洪人。歌声全是齐唱，所唱的曲子正是当年他教雅拉姆唱过的一支；只是歌词他听不真切。
歌声一起，一队人马便从更衣室里鱼贯而出，朝后殿中央行去，为首的是德高望重的古戈伊尔博士——本省音乐银行总长。总长衣饰华赡，却未戴主教冠，身上也没有任何叫人联想到英国主教或欧洲主教的东西。副总长、总管理人、副总管理人和银行的几位出纳员随即在他左右排开，鎏金错彩地立在由后殿通中殿的台阶顶上，俨然一个可观的班列。他们在这里候着，直候到歌者唱完。
连祷文——或者圣歌，或者随它叫什么——唱罢，总管理人离开总长身边，下到中殿的读经台前，宣布即将宣读《太阳之子言行录》（*Sunchild’s Sayings*）中的若干段落。也许因为按他们的新历这一天是岁首，宣读便从第一章读起，整章通读。父亲告诉我，末尾那一节他记得清清楚楚是自己说的，而且至今仍奉为真理；其余部分却几乎没有一个字出过他的口——虽然几乎处处他都认出了自己的影响。诵读者在每段之间停顿约五秒钟，很见效果；读得又慢，又极清楚。那一章如下：——

===Original===
> These are the words of the Sunchild about God and man. He said﻿—
The Head Manager now resumed his place by President Gurgoyle’s side, and the President in the name of his Majesty the King declared the temple to be hereby dedicated to the contemplation of the Sunchild and the better exposition of his teaching. This was all that was said. The reliquary was then brought forward and placed at the top of the steps leading from the apse to the nave; but the original intention of carrying it round the temple was abandoned for fear of accidents through the pressure round it of the enormous multitudes who were assembled. More singing followed of a simple but impressive kind; during this I am afraid I must own that my father, tired with his walk, dropped off into a refreshing slumber, from which he did not wake till George nudged him and told him not to snore, just as the Vice-Manager was going towards the lectern to read another chapter of the *Sunchild’s Sayings*﻿—which was as follows:﻿—
> The Sunchild also spoke to us a parable about the unwisdom of the children yet unborn, who though they know so much, yet do not know as much as they think they do.
> He said:﻿—

===Chinese===
> 这些便是太阳之子论神与人的言语。他说——
总管理人随即回到古戈伊尔总长身旁；总长以国王陛下之名宣布：此殿自即日始，奉献于对太阳之子的观想，及其教义之更完善的阐释。要说的话便只有这些。接着，圣物匣被捧上前来，安置在由后殿通中殿的台阶顶上；原先打算捧着它绕殿一周，也作罢了——生怕聚在此地的无边人海将它团团围挤，闹出差池。随后又是一阵歌唱，简单而动人；唱到其间，恕我不得不承认，我父亲因走路乏了，竟自沉沉睡去，睡得甚是香甜，直到乔治捅了捅他，叫他别打呼噜，方才醒来——那时副总管理人正走向读经台，要去宣读《太阳之子言行录》的另一章——那一章如下：——
> 太阳之子又向我们讲过一个寓言，说的是那些尚未出世的儿女的不智：他们虽然知道得那么多，却并不像自己以为的那样知道得多。
> 他说：——

===Original===
> “The unborn have knowledge of one another so long as they are unborn, and this without impediment from walls or material obstacles. The unborn children in any city form a population apart, who talk with one another and tell each other about their developmental progress.
> “They have no knowledge, and cannot even conceive the existence of anything that is not such as they are themselves. Those who have been born are to them what the dead are to us. They can see no life in them, and know no more about them than they do of any stage in their own past development other than the one through which they are passing at the moment. They do not even know that their mothers are alive﻿—much less that their mothers were once as they now are. To an embryo, its mother is simply the environment, and is looked upon much as our inorganic surroundings are by ourselves.
> “The great terror of their lives is the fear of birth﻿—that they shall have to leave the only thing that they can think of as life, and enter upon a dark unknown which is to them tantamount to annihilation.
> “Some, indeed, among them have maintained that birth is not the death which they commonly deem it, but that there is a life beyond the womb of which they as yet know nothing, and which is a million fold more truly life than anything they have yet been able even to imagine. But the greater number shake their yet unfashioned heads and say they have no evidence for this that will stand a moment’s examination.

===Chinese===
> 「未生的人只要还未出生，彼此便相识相知，墙壁与有形的障碍都阻隔不得。任一座城里的未生儿女都自成一个族群，他们互通言语，向彼此讲说自己发育的进境。
> 「他们一无所知，连凡与他们自身不同的东西的存在，都无从想象。已经出世的人，在他们看来，犹如死者之于我们。他们在那些人身上看不出生命，对他们的了解，不多于对自己发育历程中任何一个既往阶段的了解——眼下正经历的这一阶段除外。他们甚至不知道自己的母亲活着——更不知道母亲也曾同他们现在一个样。在一个胚胎看来，母亲不过是环境，看待她，同我们看待自己周围的无机环境没有两样。
> 「他们此生的大恐怖，便是怕出生——怕不得不离开他们唯一能认作生命的东西，进入一片幽暗的未知；那未知在他们看来，无异于湮灭。
> 「他们中间固然有些人坚称：出生并非他们通常认定的那种死，子宫之外另有一种生命，他们至今一无所知，而那生命比他们迄今所能设想的任何东西，都要真实百万倍。但更多的人摇着尚未成形的头颅说：这样的事，拿不出一件经得起片刻推敲的证据。

===Original===
> “ ‘Nay,’ answer the others, ‘so much work, so elaborate, so wondrous as that whereon we are now so busily engaged must have a purpose, though the purpose is beyond our grasp.’
> “ ‘Never,’ reply the first speakers; ‘our pleasure in the work is sufficient justification for it. Who has ever partaken of this life you speak of, and reentered into the womb to tell us of it? Granted that some few have pretended to have done this, but how completely have their stories broken down when subjected to the tests of sober criticism. No. When we are born we are born, and there is an end of us.’
> “But in the hour of birth, when they can no longer reenter the womb and tell the others, Behold! they find that it is not so.”
Here the reader again closed his book and resumed his place in the apse.

===Chinese===
> 「『不然，』另一些人答道，『这样的工程，这般精巧、这般奇妙的工程——我们如今正忙着营造的——必有一个目的，纵然那目的非我们所能窥。』
> 「『断乎没有，』先说话的人回道，『营造本身自有乐趣，这乐趣便是它十足的道理。谁曾尝过你所说的那种生命，重回子宫里来讲给我们听？就算有寥寥几个自称这样做过，可他们的说法一经冷静批评的检验，何等干净地败露了。不。我们生下来便是生下来了，我们到此为止。』
> 「然而到了临盆的一刻，再也回不进子宫、无从告诉旁人了——看哪！他们发觉，事情并非如此。」
读到这里，诵读者又阖上书，回到后殿自己的位置上去了。

## II To the Foot of the Pass Into Erewhon / 二、通往埃瑞洪的山口脚下

===Original===
When my father reached the colony for which he had left England some twenty-two years previously, he bought a horse, and started up country on the evening of the day after his arrival, which was, as I have said, on one of the last days of November 1890. He had taken an English saddle with him, and a couple of roomy and strongly made saddlebags. In these he packed his money, his nuggets, some tea, sugar, tobacco, salt, a flask of brandy, matches, and as many ship’s biscuits as he thought he was likely to want; he took no meat, for he could supply himself from some accommodation-house or sheep-station, when nearing the point after which he would have to begin camping out. He rolled his Erewhonian dress and small toilette necessaries inside a warm red blanket, and strapped the roll on to the front part of his saddle. On to other D’s, with which his saddle was amply provided, he strapped his Erewhonian boots, a tin pannikin, and a billy that would hold about a quart. I should, perhaps, explain to English readers that a billy is a tin can, the name for which (doubtless of French Canadian origin) is derived from the words “*faire bouillir*.” He also took with him a pair of hobbles and a small hatchet.

He spent three whole days in riding across the plains, and was struck with the very small signs of change that he could detect, but the fall in wool, and the failure, so far, to establish a frozen meat trade, had prevented any material development of the resources of the country. When he had got to the front ranges, he followed up the river next to the north of the one that he had explored years ago, and from the head waters of which he had been led to discover the only practicable pass into Erewhon. He did this, partly to avoid the terribly dangerous descent on to the bed of the more northern river, and partly to escape being seen by shepherds or bullock-drivers who might remember him.

If he had attempted to get through the gorge of this river in 1870, he would have found it impassable; but a few riverbed flats had been discovered above the gorge, on which there was now a shepherd’s hut, and on the discovery of these flats a narrow horse track had been made from one end of the gorge to the other.

===Chinese===
我父亲抵达那片殖民地——他离开英格兰已有二十二个年头——之后，便买了一匹马，在到达次日的傍晚启程向内陆进发；那一天，如我先前所说，正是 1890 年 11 月末的某一日。他随身带了一副英式马鞍和两只宽敞而结实的鞍囊。囊中装着他的钱款和金块，还有茶叶、糖、烟草、盐、一小瓶白兰地、火柴，以及他估计够用的船用硬饼；肉他并不携带，因为在临近必须扎营露宿的地点之前，沿途的客栈或牧羊场都能补给。他把埃瑞洪装束和少许盥洗必需品卷进一条暖和的红毯子里，把这卷行囊捆在马鞍前部。他的马鞍上配有充足的 D 形环，他又往其余的环上捆了埃瑞洪靴子、一只白铁缸子和一只约可容一夸脱的「比利」（billy）壶。或许我该向英国读者解释一句：所谓 billy，就是一只铁皮罐，这名字（想必源自法裔加拿大语）由「*faire bouillir*」（煮沸）两词而来。他还随身带了一副绊马索和一柄小斧。

他骑马穿过平原足足用了三天，一路上所能察觉的变化之少令他暗暗称奇；然而羊毛跌价，加上冻肉贸易迟迟未能开办，已使这个国家的资源得不到任何实质开发。抵达前沿山岭后，他便沿着一条河上溯——那条河在他多年前踏勘过的河的紧北面；当年，正是循着踏勘过的那条河的源头，他才发现通往埃瑞洪的唯一可通行的山口。他之所以如此，一半是为避开当年下到这条偏北的河床上去的那段极其危险的陡落，一半是为躲开或许还记得他的牧羊人或赶牛夫的耳目。

倘若他在 1870 年就试图溯这条河穿过峡谷，定会发现它根本无法通行；但峡谷以上后来发现了几片河滩平地，其中一片如今已盖起一座牧羊人的小屋；而随着这几片平地的发现，人们还从峡谷一端到另一端修出了一条狭窄的马道。

===Original===
He was hospitably entertained at the shepherd’s hut just mentioned, which he reached on Monday, December 1. He told the shepherd in charge of it that he had come to see if he could find traces of a large wingless bird, whose existence had been reported as having been discovered among the extreme head waters of the river.

“Be careful, sir,” said the shepherd; “the river is very dangerous; several people﻿—one only about a year ago﻿—have left this hut, and though their horses and their camps have been found, their bodies have not. When a great fresh comes down, it would carry a body out to sea in twenty-four hours.”

He evidently had no idea that there was a pass through the ranges up the river, which might explain the disappearance of an explorer.

===Chinese===
12 月 1 日，星期一，他抵达上面提到的那座牧羊小屋，受到殷勤款待。他对主管小屋的牧羊人说，自己是来查看能否寻得一种大型无翼鸟的踪迹——据报告，有人发现这种鸟存活在这条河最顶端的源头一带。

「您可要当心，先生，」牧羊人说，「这条河凶险得很。已有好几个人——其中一个不过一年前——从这间屋子出发，马匹和营帐都寻到了，尸身却始终不见。一旦大洪水冲下来，一具尸首不出二十四小时就会给带进海里。」

他显然全然不知群山之间、溯河而上有一道山口——这本可以解释一位探险者的失踪。

===Original===
Next day my father began to ascend the river. There was so much tangled growth still unburnt wherever there was room for it to grow, and so much swamp, that my father had to keep almost entirely to the riverbed﻿—and here there was a good deal of quicksand. The stones also were often large for some distance together, and he had to cross and recross streams of the river more than once, so that though he travelled all day with the exception of a couple of hours for dinner, he had not made more than some five and twenty miles when he reached a suitable camping ground, where he unsaddled his horse, hobbled him, and turned him out to feed. The grass was beginning to seed, so that though it was none too plentiful, what there was of it made excellent feed.

He lit his fire, made himself some tea, ate his cold mutton and biscuits, and lit his pipe, exactly as he had done twenty years before. There was the clear starlit sky, the rushing river, and the stunted trees on the mountainside; the woodhens cried, and the “more-pork” hooted out her two monotonous notes exactly as they had done years since; one moment, and time had so flown backwards that youth came bounding back to him with the return of his youth’s surroundings; the next, and the intervening twenty years﻿—most of them grim ones﻿—rose up mockingly before him, and the buoyancy of hope yielded to the despondency of admitted failure. By and by buoyancy reasserted itself, and, soothed by the peace and beauty of the night, he wrapped himself up in his blanket and dropped off into a dreamless slumber.

Next morning, i.e. December 3, he rose soon after dawn, bathed in a backwater of the river, got his breakfast, found his horse on the riverbed, and started as soon as he had duly packed and loaded. He had now to cross streams of the river and recross them more often than on the preceding day, and this, though his horse took well to the water, required care; for he was anxious not to wet his saddlebags, and it was only by crossing at the wide, smooth, water above a rapid, and by picking places where the river ran in two or three streams, that he could find fords where his practised eye told him that the water would not be above his horse’s belly﻿—for the river was of great volume. Fortunately, there had been a late fall of snow on the higher ranges, and the river was, for the summer season, low.

===Chinese===
翌日，我父亲开始溯河而上。凡是有空隙容草木生长之处，至今未烧过的纠结灌木比比皆是，再加上遍地的沼泽，他几乎只得完全取道河床——而河床上又有不少流沙。河床里的石头也往往一连好一段都是巨石，他不得不一次又一次地往返涉渡河中各股水流；因此，除停下吃午饭的两个钟头外，他赶了一整天路，到得一处合适的宿营地时，也不过走了二十五英里上下。他在那里卸下马鞍、绊住马腿，放它出去吃草。草正开始结籽，所以尽管并不算丰盛，现有的那些却成了上好的牧草。

他生起火，给自己沏了茶，吃了冷羊肉和硬饼，又点上烟斗——与二十年前的所为分毫不差。头顶是晴朗的星空，身旁是奔涌的河水，山腰上是矮曲的树木；林鸡啼叫，鸣声如其名的「莫波克」夜枭（more-pork）也叫着她那两声单调的咕鸣，一切都与多年以前一般无二。一时之间，时光如此倒流，青春年少的旧境随四周景物一齐欢蹦着回到他身上；转瞬间，其间相隔的二十年——大半是严峻的年月——又嘲弄般地耸立在眼前，希望的昂扬让位于自认失败后的沮丧。渐渐地，那股昂扬重又抬头；在夜的安宁与美好的抚慰下，他裹紧毯子，坠入了无梦的酣睡。

次日清晨，即 12 月 3 日，破晓不久他便起身，在河湾的回流死水里洗了个澡，用过早饭，在河床上找回马匹，收拾捆扎停当后随即上路。这一天，他往返涉渡河中各股水流的次数比前一天更频；马虽颇肯下水，此事仍需谨慎：他唯恐打湿鞍囊，只有拣急流上方那片宽阔平缓的水面渡河，并挑取河道分成两三股的地方，才能凭老练的眼光觅得水深不致没过马腹的涉口——因为这条河水量极大。所幸更高的山岭上新近落过一场晚雪，就夏季而论，河水还算浅。

===Original===
Towards evening, having travelled, so far as he could guess, some twenty or five and twenty miles (for he had made another mid day halt), he reached the place, which he easily recognised, as that where he had camped before crossing to the pass that led into Erewhon. It was the last piece of ground that could be called a flat (though it was in reality only the sloping delta of a stream that descended from the pass) before reaching a large glacier that had encroached on the riverbed, which it traversed at right angles for a considerable distance.

Here he again camped, hobbled his horse, and turned him adrift, hoping that he might again find him some two or three months hence, for there was a good deal of sweet grass here and there, with sow-thistle and anise; and the coarse tussock grass would be in full seed shortly, which alone would keep him going for as long a time as my father expected to be away. Little did he think that he should want him again so shortly.

Having attended to his horse, he got his supper, and while smoking his pipe congratulated himself on the way in which something had smoothed away all the obstacles that had so nearly baffled him on his earlier journey. Was he being lured on to his destruction by some malicious fiend, or befriended by one who had compassion on him and wished him well? His naturally sanguine temperament inclined him to adopt the friendly spirit theory, in the peace of which he again laid himself down to rest, and slept soundly from dark till dawn.

===Chinese===
将近傍晚，他估摸着已走了二十到二十五英里（因为中午又歇过一次脚），抵达一处他毫不费力便认出的地方——正是当年他在翻越通往埃瑞洪的山口之前扎营的所在。这是最后一片还称得上平地的地方（其实不过是自山口倾泻而下的一条溪流所形成的斜坡形三角洲），再往前，便是一道大片侵入河床的冰川，它以直角横贯河床，绵延颇远。

他在此再度扎营，绊上马腿，任马自行走食，指望两三个月后还能把它寻回，因为这一带零星长着不少甜草，还有苦苣菜和茴香；粗硬的丛生草不久便将结满籽，单是这一样，就足够马儿撑过他预计离开的时日。他万没想到，这么快便又要用它了。

照料好马之后，他吃了晚饭，一面抽着烟斗，一面暗自庆幸：冥冥中似乎有什么，把当年险些难倒他的重重障碍一一抚平了。这是某个恶毒的魔灵在诱他走向毁灭呢，还是一位怜悯他、愿他好的善灵在加以护佑？他天性乐观，便倾向于这善灵之说；他怀着这份安宁重新躺下歇息，从天黑酣睡到破晓。

===Original===
In the morning, though the water was somewhat icy, he again bathed, and then put on his Erewhonian boots and dress. He stowed his European clothes, with some difficulty, into his saddlebags. Herein also he left his case full of English sovereigns, his spare pipes, his purse, which contained two pounds in gold and seven or eight shillings, part of his stock of tobacco, and whatever provision was left him, except the meat﻿—which he left for sundry hawks and parrots that were eyeing his proceedings apparently without fear of man. His nuggets he concealed in the secret pockets of which I have already spoken, keeping one bag alone accessible.

He had had his hair and beard cut short on shipboard the day before he landed. These he now dyed with a dye that he had brought from England, and which in a few minutes turned them very nearly black. He also stained his face and hands deep brown. He hung his saddle and bridle, his English boots, and his saddlebags on the highest bough that he could reach, and made them fairly fast with strips of flax leaf, for there was some stunted flax growing on the ground where he had camped. He feared that, do what he might, they would not escape the inquisitive thievishness of the parrots, whose strong beaks could easily cut leather; but he could do nothing more. It occurs to me, though my father never told me so, that it was perhaps with a view to these birds that he had chosen to put his English sovereigns into a metal box, with a clasp to it which would defy them.

He made a roll of his blanket, and slung it over his shoulder; he also took his pipe, tobacco, a little tea, a few ship’s biscuits, and his billy and pannikin; matches and salt go without saying. When he had thus ordered everything as nearly to his satisfaction as he could, he looked at his watch for the last time, as he believed, till many weeks should have gone by, and found it to be about seven o’clock. Remembering what trouble it had got him into years before, he took down his saddlebags, reopened them, and put the watch inside. He then set himself to climb the mountain side, towards the saddle on which he had seen the statues.

===Chinese===
早晨，尽管河水颇有几分冰凉，他又洗了个澡，然后穿上埃瑞洪靴子和埃瑞洪装束。他的欧洲衣裳费了些劲才塞进鞍囊。他还把那只装满英国金镑的匣子、备用烟斗、钱袋——内有两英镑金币和七八先令——一部分烟草，以及所剩的各色食品留在了囊中，唯独肉除外——那是留给几只鹰隼和鹦鹉的，它们正盯视着他的一举一动，似乎全不怕人。金块则藏进我已说过的那些暗袋里，只留一袋取用方便。

登陆前一天，他已在船上把头发和须髯剪短。此刻他用从英国带来的染料把须发染了，不出几分钟便几乎染成乌黑。他又把脸和双手涂成深褐色。他把马鞍、辔头、英国靴子和鞍囊挂上他够得着的最高树枝，用撕成条的亚麻叶缚得牢牢实实——他扎营的地面上正长着几丛矮小的亚麻。他担心无论如何，这些东西也逃不过鹦鹉们好奇而成性的偷窃，它们的利喙轻易便能啄断皮革；可他再无别的办法。我忽然想到——尽管父亲从未对我明言——他把英国金镑装进一只带搭扣的金属匣子，叫那些鸟儿无从啄开，或许正是提防这些家伙。

他把毯子卷成一卷，挎在肩上；又带上烟斗、烟草、一点茶叶、几块船用硬饼，还有他的比利壶和白铁缸子；火柴和盐自不待言。诸事安排得大致如意之后，他看了看表——他相信，此后许多个星期之内这将是最后一次——时辰约莫在七点钟。想起这块表多年前给他招来的麻烦，他取下鞍囊，重新打开，把表放了进去。随后他便动身攀登山坡，朝着他当年望见群像的那道鞍状山脊而去。

## I Ups and Downs of Fortune—My Father Starts for Erewhon / 一、命运沉浮——父亲动身前往埃瑞洪

===Original===
Before telling the story of my father’s second visit to the remarkable country which he discovered now some thirty years since, I should perhaps say a few words about his career between the publication of his book in 1872, and his death in the early summer of 1891. I shall thus touch briefly on the causes that occasioned his failure to maintain that hold on the public which he had apparently secured at first.

His book, as the reader may perhaps know, was published anonymously, and my poor father used to ascribe the acclamation with which it was received, to the fact that no one knew who it might not have been written by. *Omne ignotum pro magnifico*, and during its month of anonymity the book was a frequent topic of appreciative comment in good literary circles. Almost coincidently with the discovery that he was a mere nobody, people began to feel that their admiration had been too hastily bestowed, and before long opinion turned all the more seriously against him for this very reason. The subscription, to which the Lord Mayor had at first given his cordial support, was curtly announced as closed before it had been opened a week; it had met with so little success that I will not specify the amount eventually handed over, not without protest, to my father; small, however, as it was, he narrowly escaped being prosecuted for trying to obtain money under false pretences.

The Geographical Society, which had for a few days received him with open arms, was among the first to turn upon him﻿—not, so far as I can ascertain, on account of the mystery in which he had enshrouded the exact whereabouts of Erewhon, nor yet by reason of its being persistently alleged that he was subject to frequent attacks of alcoholic poisoning﻿—but through his own want of tact, and a highly-strung nervous state, which led him to attach too much importance to his own discoveries, and not enough to those of other people. This, at least, was my father’s version of the matter, as I heard it from his own lips in the later years of his life.

“I was still very young,” he said to me, “and my mind was more or less unhinged by the strangeness and peril of my adventures.” Be this as it may, I fear there is no doubt that he was injudicious; and an ounce of judgement is worth a pound of discovery.

===Chinese===
在讲述我父亲第二回造访那个非同寻常的国度——约莫三十年前由他发现的国度——之前，我或许应当先用几句话，交代他自 1872 年出书至 1891 年初夏去世之间的际遇；如此也可约略说明，他起初似乎已然赢得的那种对公众的把握，后来因何未能保持。

读者或许知道，他这本书是匿名出版的；而我那可怜的父亲，常把此书受到的喝彩归因于一点：谁也不知道它未必出自哪位名家之手。*Omne ignotum pro magnifico*（凡属未知，皆自不凡），在匿名的那个月里，这本书在上乘的文学圈子里，屡屡是受人赏叹的谈资。可几乎就在人们发觉他不过是个无名之辈的同时，大家便开始觉得自己的倾倒未免下得太急；不久，舆论更恰恰因此愈发郑重地转而与他为敌。认捐一事——伦敦市长（the Lord Mayor）起初还曾热诚支持——开办未满一星期，便由一纸简短通告宣布结束；其成绩之寥寥，以致最终那笔——并非全无异议地——交到我父亲手里的款子究竟几何，我就不去细说了；然而纵使数目区区，他还险些因「假托虚辞骗取钱财」的罪名被提起公诉。

地理学会（the Geographical Society）有几天工夫曾对他敞怀接纳，却在最早调头攻击他的人们之列——据我所能查考，这既不是因为他把埃瑞洪（Erewhon）的确切下落罩进重重迷雾，也不是因为人们一再指称他动辄「酒精中毒」发作，而是由于他自己不够圆通，加之神经高度紧张，以致过分看重自己的发现，而对别人的发现又看重得太少。至少，这是我父亲对这件事的说法，是我在他晚年亲耳听来的。

「那时我还很年轻，」他对我说，「冒险经历的离奇与凶险，已把我的心神弄得多少有些错乱。」话虽如此，恐怕毫无疑义的是，他行事确实欠判断；而一盎司的判断力，抵得上一磅的发现。

===Original===
Hence, in a surprisingly short time, he found himself dropped even by those who had taken him up most warmly, and had done most to find him that employment as a writer of religious tracts on which his livelihood was then dependent. The discredit, however, into which my father fell, had the effect of deterring any considerable number of people from trying to rediscover Erewhon, and thus caused it to remain as unknown to geographers in general as though it had never been found. A few shepherds and cadets at upcountry stations had, indeed, tried to follow in my father’s footsteps, during the time when his book was still being taken seriously; but they had most of them returned, unable to face the difficulties that had opposed them. Some few, however, had not returned, and though search was made for them, their bodies had not been found. When he reached Erewhon on his second visit, my father learned that others had attempted to visit the country more recently﻿—probably quite independently of his own book; and before he had himself been in it many hours he gathered what the fate of these poor fellows doubtless was.

Another reason that made it more easy for Erewhon to remain unknown, was the fact that the more mountainous districts, though repeatedly prospected for gold, had been pronounced non-auriferous, and as there was no sheep or cattle country, save a few riverbed flats above the upper gorges of any of the rivers, and no game to tempt the sportsman, there was nothing to induce people to penetrate into the fastnesses of the great snowy range. No more, therefore, being heard of Erewhon, my father’s book came to be regarded as a mere work of fiction, and I have heard quite recently of its having been seen on a secondhand bookstall, marked “6d. very readable.”

Though there was no truth in the stories about my father’s being subject to attacks of alcoholic poisoning, yet, during the first few years after his return to England, his occasional fits of ungovernable excitement gave some colour to the opinion that much of what he said he had seen and done might be only subjectively true. I refer more particularly to his interview with Chowbok in the wool-shed, and his highly coloured description of the statues on the top of the pass leading into Erewhon. These were soon set down as forgeries of delirium, and it was maliciously urged, that though in his book he had only admitted having taken “two or three bottles of brandy” with him, he had probably taken at least a dozen; and that if on the night before he reached the statues he had “only four ounces of brandy” left, he must have been drinking heavily for the preceding fortnight or three weeks. Those who read the following pages will, I think, reject all idea that my father was in a state of delirium, not without surprise that anyone should have ever entertained it.

It was Chowbok who, if he did not originate these calumnies, did much to disseminate and gain credence for them. He remained in England for some years, and never tired of doing what he could to disparage my father. The cunning creature had ingratiated himself with our leading religious societies, especially with the more evangelical among them. Whatever doubt there might be about his sincerity, there was none about his colour, and a coloured convert in those days was more than Exeter Hall could resist. Chowbok saw that there was no room for him and for my father, and declared my poor father’s story to be almost wholly false. It was true, he said, that he and my father had explored the headwaters of the river described in his book, but he denied that my father had gone on without him, and he named the river as one distant by many thousands of miles from the one it really was. He said that after about a fortnight he had returned in company with my father, who by that time had become incapacitated for further travel. At this point he would shrug his shoulders, look mysterious, and thus say “alcoholic poisoning” even more effectively than if he had uttered the words themselves. For a man’s tongue lies often in his shoulders.

===Chinese===
于是，在短得出奇的时间里，他便发觉自己连那些当初最热忱抬举他、最卖力替他谋得宗教小册子写手那份差事的人——他当时的生计正赖于此——也把他撇下了。不过，我父亲的失势倒也有个效果：它吓退了为数可观的人，使他们不再试图重新发现埃瑞洪，于是这个国度照旧不为一般地理学家所知，仿佛从未被发现过。在他的书还被认真看待的那段日子里，确有几位内地牧场的牧羊人和见习牧工想循他的足迹走一遭，但多半半途而返，敌不过横亘面前的重重困难。然而也有寥寥几个没有回来；虽经搜寻，尸骨始终无着。待到第二次访问抵达埃瑞洪时，我父亲才得知，新近另有别人也试图进入那个国度——多半同他的书毫不相干；而他自己进入国中没有多少个钟头，就已明白这些可怜家伙的下场多半如何。

埃瑞洪之所以易于继续不为人知，还有一层缘故：多山地带虽经一再勘探金矿，却已断定不含黄金；又因除了几条河流上游峡谷以上的小片河谷滩地外，既没有牧放牛羊之乡，也没有引动猎兴的野味，便再没有什么能诱人深入大雪山脉的重重天险。此后埃瑞洪既再无音讯，我父亲的书便渐渐被看作单纯的虚构之作；就在最近，我还听说有人在旧书摊上见到它，标着「6 便士，颇为可读」。

有关我父亲动辄「酒精中毒」的种种传闻固然全属子虚，但在他回到英国后的最初几年里，他间或发作、不能自持的亢奋，却给一种见解添了几分颜色：他自称见过做过的事情，许多恐怕只是主观上的真实。我特别指的是他在羊毛棚里与乔博克（Chowbok）的那番对答，以及他对进入埃瑞洪的山口顶端那些雕像的浓墨重彩的描写。这些不久便被断为谵妄中造出的赝品；更有人恶意地絮絮不休：他在书里虽只承认随身带了「两三瓶白兰地」，实际带的多半至少有一打；倘若抵达雕像的前一夜他「只剩四盎司白兰地」，那么在此之前的两个星期乃至三个星期里，他必定喝得很凶。我想，读罢以下各页的读者都会断然排除我父亲当时处于谵妄状态的想法，并且不免诧异，这种想法当初怎么竟会有人当过真。

这些诽谤即便并非乔博克所首创，散播它们并使人信服的也大半是他。他在英国一住数年，孜孜不倦地做一切能贬损我父亲的事。这个狡黠的家伙巴结上了我们几家领风的宗教团体，尤其是其中更热中福音派的那几家。他的虔诚尽可存疑，他的肤色却无可置疑；而在那些年头，一位有色人种的皈依者，是埃克塞特大厅（Exeter Hall）里诸公所抵挡不住的。乔博克看得分明，他与我父亲势不两立，便宣称我那可怜的父亲所讲的经历几乎全属虚妄。他说，他和我的确——嗯——和他和我父亲曾一同探察书中所写的那条河的源头，这话不假；但他矢口否认我父亲曾丢下他独自前行，还把那条河说成远在真正那条河数千英里之外的另一条河。他说，约莫两个星期之后，他便和我父亲一同折返了，因为后者那时已不堪再行。讲到这里，他便耸一耸肩，神色诡秘，这样一来，「酒精中毒」四个字不吐自明，比直说出来还有效。因为一个人的舌头，往往就长在他的肩膀上。

===Original===
Readers of my father’s book will remember that Chowbok had given a very different version when he had returned to his employer’s station; but Time and Distance afford cover under which falsehood can often do truth to death securely.

I never understood why my father did not bring my mother forward to confirm his story. He may have done so while I was too young to know anything about it. But when people have made up their minds, they are impatient of further evidence; my mother, moreover, was of a very retiring disposition. The Italians say:﻿—

> “Chi lontano va ammogliare

 Sarà ingannato, o vorrà ingannare.”

“If a man goes far afield for a wife, he will be deceived﻿—or means deceiving.” The proverb is as true for women as for men, and my mother was never quite happy in her new surroundings. Wilfully deceived she assuredly was not, but she could not accustom herself to English modes of thought; indeed she never even nearly mastered our language; my father always talked with her in Erewhonian, and so did I, for as a child she had taught me to do so, and I was as fluent with her language as with my father’s. In this respect she often told me I could pass myself off anywhere in Erewhon as a native; I shared also her personal appearance, for though not wholly unlike my father, I had taken more closely after my mother. In mind, if I may venture to say so, I believe I was more like my father.

===Chinese===
读过家父之书的读者当会记得，乔博克回到他雇主的牧场时，讲的可是截然不同的另一套；然而时间和距离提供了掩体，谎言大可躲在其后，从容不迫地把真理置于死地。

我一直不明白，我父亲为什么不把我母亲请出来印证他的说法。在我年纪尚小、一无所知的时候，他也许这样做过。但人们既已拿定主意，便再容不得什么证据；何况我母亲天性羞怯，深居简出。意大利人有言：——

> “Chi lontano va ammogliare

 Sarà ingannato, o vorrà ingannare.”

「谁若远走他乡去娶妻，他不是受骗，便是存心骗人。」这条谚语用于女人同用于男人一样灵验；而我母亲在自己的新环境里，就从来不曾真正快活过。说她是有意受骗，那断然不是；可她怎么也习惯不了英国的思维方式；说实话，我们的语言她连近似精通都谈不上。父亲同她交谈始终用埃瑞洪语，我也是；因为孩提时候，是她教会我这样说的，她的话我讲得同我父亲的母语一样流利。她常说，单凭这一点，我在埃瑞洪随便哪里都能冒充本地人。我的容貌也承袭了她：虽说我同父亲并非毫无相像之处，我却是更像母亲的那一个。至于心性——容我斗胆一言——我相信自己更像父亲。

===Original===
I may as well here inform the reader that I was born at the end of September 1871, and was christened John, after my grandfather. From what I have said above he will readily believe that my earliest experiences were somewhat squalid. Memories of childhood rush vividly upon me when I pass through a low London alley, and catch the faint sickly smell that pervades it﻿—half paraffin, half black-currants, but wholly something very different. I have a fancy that we lived in Blackmoor Street, off Drury Lane. My father, when first I knew of his doing anything at all, supported my mother and myself by drawing pictures with coloured chalks upon the pavement; I used sometimes to watch him, and marvel at the skill with which he represented fogs, floods, and fires. These three “f’s,” he would say, were his three best friends, for they were easy to do and brought in halfpence freely. The return of the dove to the ark was his favourite subject. Such a little ark, on such a hazy morning, and such a little pigeon﻿—the rest of the picture being cheap sky, and still cheaper sea; nothing, I have often heard him say, was more popular than this with his clients. He held it to be his masterpiece, but would add with some naivete that he considered himself a public benefactor for carrying it out in such perishable fashion. “At any rate,” he would say, “no one can bequeath one of my many replicas to the nation.”

I never learned how much my father earned by his profession, but it must have been something considerable, for we always had enough to eat and drink; I imagine that he did better than many a struggling artist with more ambitious aims. He was strictly temperate during all the time that I knew anything about him, but he was not a teetotaler; I never saw any of the fits of nervous excitement which in his earlier years had done so much to wreck him. In the evenings, and on days when the state of the pavement did not permit him to work, he took great pains with my education, which he could very well do, for as a boy he had been in the sixth form of one of our foremost public schools. I found him a patient, kindly instructor, while to my mother he was a model husband. Whatever others may have said about him, I can never think of him without very affectionate respect.

Things went on quietly enough, as above indicated, till I was about fourteen, when by a freak of fortune my father became suddenly affluent. A brother of his father’s had emigrated to Australia in 1851, and had amassed great wealth. We knew of his existence, but there had been no intercourse between him and my father, and we did not even know that he was rich and unmarried. He died intestate towards the end of 1885, and my father was the only relative he had, except, of course, myself, for both my father’s sisters had died young, and without leaving children.

===Chinese===
我不妨在此告诉读者：我生于 1871 年 9 月底，受洗取名约翰（John），随的是祖父的名字。由上文所说，读者自会相信，我最早的阅历是相当寒碜的。每当我走过伦敦的一条陋巷，闻见那弥漫全巷、微弱而发闷的气味——一半是石蜡，一半是黑醋栗，却又整个儿是另一种东西——童年的记忆便鲜明地涌上心头。我恍惚记得，我们住在德鲁里巷（Drury Lane）旁的布莱克穆尔街（Blackmoor Street）。在我最早知道父亲操持任何营生的时候，他是靠用彩色粉笔在人行道上作画，养活我母亲和我；我有时在旁看他，惊异于他画雾、画洪水、画火灾的手段。这三个「f」——雾、洪水、火灾——他常说，是他的三位至交，因为画来省事，进账的半便士铜子儿也源源不绝。鸽子飞回方舟，是他最爱画的题材：那么小的一只方舟，配那么一个雾蒙蒙的早晨，再添那么小的一只鸽子——画的其余部分，便是廉价的天空，和更加廉价的海；我常听他说，在他的主顾中间，再没有比这更受欢迎的了。他把这幅画看作自己的压卷之作，只是又会带着几分天真补上一句：能以这样易于朽坏的方式将它绘制出来，他认为自己简直算得公众的恩人。「无论如何，」他会说，「谁也没法把我这许多复制品当中的一幅，遗赠给国家。」

我始终不曾弄清父亲靠这门营生挣得几何，但想必颇为可观，因为我们吃用从来不缺；我想，比起许多志向更大而挣扎度日的艺术家，他混得还算好的。在我对世事略有所知的全部岁月里，他都严格节制，却并非滴酒不沾的戒酒之人；他早年那些大大坑害了他的神经亢奋发作，我一次也不曾见过。到了晚上，以及路面状况不容他做工的日子，他便在我的功课上大下功夫——这他完全办得到，因为少年时，他在我们数一数二的公学里念到过六年级。在我眼里，他是位耐心而和善的老师；对我母亲，他则是位模范丈夫。不论旁人说过他什么，我一想起他，心里便涌起深情的敬意。

如上所述，日子一直过得相当平静，直到我十四岁上下，命运忽发奇想，我父亲骤然阔绰起来。他的一位伯父 1851 年移居澳大利亚，攒下了万贯家财。我们知道有这么一个人，但他同我父亲素无往来；我们甚至不知道他既有家财，又未娶亲。他死于 1885 年末，未曾立下遗嘱；撇开我不算，我父亲是他唯一的亲属，因为我父亲的那两个姐妹都年纪轻轻就死了，又都没有留下子女。

===Original===
The solicitor through whom the news reached us was, happily, a man of the highest integrity, and also very sensible and kind. He was a Mr. Alfred Emery Cathie, of 15 Clifford’s Inn, EC, and my father placed himself unreservedly in his hands. I was at once sent to a first-rate school, and such pains had my father taken with me that I was placed in a higher form than might have been expected considering my age. The way in which he had taught me had prevented my feeling any dislike for study; I therefore stuck fairly well to my books, while not neglecting the games which are so important a part of healthy education. Everything went well with me, both as regards masters and schoolfellows; nevertheless, I was declared to be of a highly nervous and imaginative temperament, and the school doctor more than once urged our headmaster not to push me forward too rapidly﻿—for which I have ever since held myself his debtor.

Early in 1890, I being then home from Oxford (where I had been entered in the preceding year), my mother died; not so much from active illness, as from what was in reality a kind of *maladie du pays*. All along she had felt herself an exile, and though she had borne up wonderfully during my father’s long struggle with adversity, she began to break as soon as prosperity had removed the necessity for exertion on her own part.

My father could never divest himself of the feeling that he had wrecked her life by inducing her to share her lot with his own; to say that he was stricken with remorse on losing her is not enough; he had been so stricken almost from the first year of his marriage; on her death he was haunted by the wrong he accused himself﻿—as it seems to me very unjustly﻿—of having done her, for it was neither his fault nor hers﻿—it was Atè.

===Chinese===
幸好，替我们把消息传来的那位律师，是个操守极好的人，又极明白事理、心地仁厚。他名叫阿尔弗雷德·埃默里·凯西（Mr. Alfred Emery Cathie），事务所设在伦敦 EC 区克利福德律师学院（Clifford's Inn）15 号，我父亲便把自己毫无保留地托付给了他。我随即被送进一所一流的学校；父亲从前在我身上下过那么多功夫，以致我编入的年级，比按年龄所能指望的还高了一级。他教我的那套法子，使我从不觉得读书可厌，因此书本我抓得还算牢，同时也不曾荒废游戏——那是健康教育中顶要紧的一环。无论对师长还是对同学，我都处处顺遂；尽管如此，人们还是断言我天生神经过敏、富于想象，校医还不止一次劝告校长，不要把我催逼得太急——为这一点，我至今自认欠着他一份情。

1890 年初，我正从牛津回家——我是前一年入学注册的——母亲去世了；与其说是害了什么实打实的病症，不如说是死于一种实际上乃是乡愁的毛病——*maladie du pays*。她始终自觉是个流放之人；在父亲长年同厄运搏斗的日子里，她支撑得极其出色；可家境一旦宽裕、她本人无须再勉力操劳，她反倒渐渐垮了。

我父亲永远摆脱不掉一种感觉：是他劝母亲把她的命运同他自己的拴在一起，才毁了她的这一生。说他失去她之后才痛悔交加，犹嫌不足；几乎从成婚的头一年起，他便受着这种痛悔的煎熬；她一去世，他更被自认亏待了她的那桩罪过缠住不放——依我看，这实在冤枉——因为那既不是他的错，也不是她的错——那是阿忒（Atè）。

===Original===
His unrest soon assumed the form of a burning desire to revisit the country in which he and my mother had been happier together than perhaps they ever again were. I had often heard him betray a hankering after a return to Erewhon, disguised so that no one should recognise him; but as long as my mother lived he would not leave her. When death had taken her from him, he so evidently stood in need of a complete change of scene, that even those friends who had most strongly dissuaded him from what they deemed a madcap enterprise, thought it better to leave him to himself. It would have mattered little how much they tried to dissuade him, for before long his passionate longing for the journey became so overmastering that nothing short of restraint in prison or a madhouse could have stayed his going; but we were not easy about him. “He had better go,” said Mr. Cathie to me, when I was at home for the Easter vacation, “and get it over. He is not well, but he is still in the prime of life; doubtless he will come back with renewed health and will settle down to a quiet home life again.”

This, however, was not said till it had become plain that in a few days my father would be on his way. He had made a new will, and left an ample power of attorney with Mr. Cathie﻿—or, as we always called him, Alfred﻿—who was to supply me with whatever money I wanted; he had put all other matters in order in case anything should happen to prevent his ever returning, and he set out on October 1, 1890, more composed and cheerful than I had seen him for some time past.

I had not realised how serious the danger to my father would be if he were recognised while he was in Erewhon, for I am ashamed to say that I had not yet read his book. I had heard over and over again of his flight with my mother in the balloon, and had long since read his few opening chapters, but I had found, as a boy naturally would, that the succeeding pages were a little dull, and soon put the book aside. My father, indeed, repeatedly urged me not to read it, for he said there was much in it﻿—more especially in the earlier chapters, which I had alone found interesting﻿—that he would gladly cancel if he could. “But there!” he had said with a laugh, “what does it matter?”

===Chinese===
他的不安不久便化作一股炽切的愿望：想重访那个国度——在那里，他和母亲两人厮守的时光，兴许比此后任何时候都更快活。我不止一次听他露出想回埃瑞洪的意思，只是须乔装改扮，免得叫人认出来；但母亲在世一日，他便不肯离开她。死神把她夺去之后，他显然太需要彻底换一换环境，就连那些最竭力劝阻这桩——在他们看来——孟浪之举的朋友，也觉得不如由他去了。其实他们再劝也无济于事：不久，他对这趟行程的热望便炽烈得难以驾驭，除非把他关进监狱或疯人院，什么也拦不住他动身；不过我们心里总不踏实。「还是让他去吧，」我复活节假期在家时，凯西先生对我说，「去了却这桩心事。他身子欠安，可正当盛年；毫无疑问，他会带着复元的健康回来，重新安顿下来，再过安稳的家庭生活。」

不过，凯西先生说这话时，事情已明摆着：再过几天，我父亲就要上路了。他新立了遗嘱，又给凯西先生——我们一向管他叫阿尔弗雷德——留下了一份宽泛的授权委托书，由他供给我需要的任何款项；其余各项事务，他也一一料理停当，以防万一有什么变故，叫他从此回不来。1890 年 10 月 1 日，他动身了，神态比我此前一个时期所见的都更平静、更快活。

我当时并不明白，父亲倘若在埃瑞洪被人认出，会陷入何等严重的危险；说来惭愧，那时我还没有读过他的书。他带着母亲乘气球出逃的故事，我听过不知多少遍；开头寥寥几章我也早已读过，但正如一个半大孩子自然而然会有的反应，我觉得后面的篇幅有些乏味，很快便把书撂下了。说真的，父亲倒是一再劝我别去读它；他说书里有许多东西——尤其在我唯一觉得有趣的那开头几章里——只要办得到，他巴不得统统取消。「可是算了！」他曾笑着说道，「这又有什么要紧？」

===Original===
He had hardly left, before I read his book from end to end, and, on having done so, not only appreciated the risks that he would have to run, but was struck with the wide difference between his character as he had himself portrayed it, and the estimate I had formed of it from personal knowledge. When, on his return, he detailed to me his adventures, the account he gave of what he had said and done corresponded with my own ideas concerning him; but I doubt not the reader will see that the twenty years between his first and second visit had modified him even more than so long an interval might be expected to do.

I heard from him repeatedly during the first two months of his absence, and was surprised to find that he had stayed for a week or ten days at more than one place of call on his outward journey. On November 26 he wrote from the port whence he was to start for Erewhon, seemingly in good health and spirits; and on December 27, 1891, he telegraphed for a hundred pounds to be wired out to him at this same port. This puzzled both Mr. Cathie and myself, for the interval between November 26 and December 27 seemed too short to admit of his having paid his visit to Erewhon and returned; as, moreover, he had added the words, “Coming home,” we rather hoped that he had abandoned his intention of going there.

We were also surprised at his wanting so much money, for he had taken a hundred pounds in gold, which from some fancy, he had stowed in a small silver jewel-box that he had given my mother not long before she died. He had also taken a hundred pounds worth of gold nuggets, which he had intended to sell in Erewhon so as to provide himself with money when he got there.

===Chinese===
他前脚刚走，我便把他的书从头到尾读了一遍；读完之后，不但领会了他势必要冒的种种风险，还惊觉他在书中给自己描画的性格，同我凭亲身交往所形成的判断，相去何其悬殊。待他归来向我细述历险时，他对自身言行的交代，同我对他的看法正相吻合；不过读者自会看出，第一回与第二回访问之间的那二十年，把他改变的程度，比这样一段长间隔通常所能改变的还要深。

他离家后的头两个月，我屡屡收到他的信；使我不解的是，他竟在去程不止一处停靠之地，盘桓了七日乃至十日。11 月 26 日，他从将启程赴埃瑞洪的那个港口来信，健康与兴致似乎都好；1891 年 12 月 27 日，他却来电报，要人把一百英镑电汇到同一港口给他。这叫凯西先生和我都犯了琢磨：11 月 26 日至 12 月 27 日之间，似乎太短，容不得他去一趟埃瑞洪再回来；何况电文里还添了「即归」两个字，我们倒宁可指望，他已打消了去那边的念头。

他要这么多钱，也叫我们吃惊：因为他带了一百英镑金币——不知出于什么怪念头——收在一只小小的银质首饰盒里，那盒子是他去世前不久送给我母亲的。他还带了价值一百英镑的天然金块，打算到埃瑞洪变卖，好让自己到达之后手头有钱。

===Original===
I should explain that these nuggets would be worth in Erewhon fully ten times as much as they would in Europe, owing to the great scarcity of gold in that country. The Erewhonian coinage is entirely silver﻿—which is abundant, and worth much what it is in England﻿—or copper, which is also plentiful; but what we should call five pounds’ worth of silver money would not buy more than one of our half-sovereigns in gold.

He had put his nuggets into ten brown holland bags, and he had had secret pockets made for the old Erewhonian dress which he had worn when he escaped, so that he need never have more than one bag of nuggets accessible at a time. He was not likely, therefore, to have been robbed. His passage to the port above referred to had been paid before he started, and it seemed impossible that a man of his very inexpensive habits should have spent two hundred pounds in a single month﻿—for the nuggets would be immediately convertible in an English colony. There was nothing, however, to be done but to cable out the money and wait my father’s arrival.

Returning for a moment to my father’s old Erewhonian dress, I should say that he had preserved it simply as a memento and without any idea that he should again want it. It was not the court dress that had been provided for him on the occasion of his visit to the king and queen, but the everyday clothing that he had been ordered to wear when he was put in prison, though his English coat, waistcoat, and trousers had been allowed to remain in his own possession. These, I had seen from his book, had been presented by him to the queen (with the exception of two buttons, which he had given to Yram as a keepsake), and had been preserved by her displayed upon a wooden dummy. The dress in which he escaped had been soiled during the hours that he and my mother had been in the sea, and had also suffered from neglect during the years of his poverty; but he wished to pass himself off as a common peasant or workingman, so he preferred to have it set in order as might best be done, rather than copied.

===Chinese===
这里应当说明：由于该国黄金奇缺，这些金块在埃瑞洪的价值，足有在欧洲的十倍。埃瑞洪的币制纯用白银——白银甚丰，价值同英国的相去无几——或用铜，铜也很充裕；可是我们所谓值五英镑的银钱，还买不来我们的半镑金币一枚。

他把金块分装在十只棕色亚麻布小袋里，又给那身他当年出逃时穿的旧埃瑞洪衣裳缝了些暗袋，这样，能随手拿到的金块，一次至多一袋。因此，他不大可能遭劫。他去上述港口的旅费在启程前已经付讫；像他这样习性极其俭省的人，一个月里花掉两百英镑，看来断无可能——因为那些金块，到了英国人的殖民地立时可以兑成现钱。只是我们别无良策，只好把钱电汇过去，静候我父亲归来。

回头再说我父亲那身旧埃瑞洪衣裳。应当交代一句：他保存它，原只为留个纪念，并没想到还会有再需要它的一天。那并不是他觐见国王和王后时替他备下的礼服，而是他下狱时奉命穿用的家常衣裳——虽说他那身英国外套、背心和裤子，倒获准留在自己手里。据我从他书中所见，那身英国衣裳后来由他呈献给了王后（只有两粒纽扣不在此数，那是他送给雅拉姆（Yram）留作纪念的），由王后陈列在一具木制假人上保存。他出逃时穿的那身衣裳，在他和母亲泡在海里的那几个钟头里浸污了，其后在贫困的年月里又因疏于照料而更受损残；但他既想把自己装扮成寻常的庄稼汉或做工的人，便宁可教人把它尽量收拾整饬，而不愿照样仿制一身。

===Original===
So cautious was he in the matter of dress that he took with him the boots he had worn on leaving Erewhon, lest the foreign make of his English boots should arouse suspicion. They were nearly new, and when he had had them softened and well greased, he found he could still wear them quite comfortably.

But to return. He reached home late at night one day at the beginning of February, and a glance was enough to show that he was an altered man. “What is the matter?” said I, shocked at his appearance. “Did you go to Erewhon, and were you ill-treated there?”

“I went to Erewhon,” he said, “and I was not ill-treated there, but I have been so shaken that I fear I shall quite lose my reason. Do not ask me more now. I will tell you about it all tomorrow. Let me have something to eat, and go to bed.”

When we met at breakfast next morning, he greeted me with all his usual warmth of affection, but he was still taciturn. “I will begin to tell you about it,” he said, “after breakfast. Where is your dear mother? How was it that I have﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
他在衣着一事上小心到这般地步：连离开埃瑞洪时穿的那双靴子也一并带上了，唯恐他那双英国靴子的外国做工惹人疑心。那双靴子几乎还是新的；教人把它回软、上足了油之后，他发觉穿着依旧十分合脚。

话归正传。2 月上旬的一天深夜，他到了家；只消看上一眼，便足以知道他已判若两人。「出什么事了？」我说，他的模样使我惊骇，「你是去了埃瑞洪吗？在那边受了虐待吗？」

「我是去了埃瑞洪，」他说，「在那边也没有受虐待；只是我受了这样的震荡，只怕神志就要完全丧失了。现在别再问我。明天我会把一切都讲给你听。给我弄点吃的，我这就去睡。」

第二天早晨用早饭时再见面，他一如往常那般热络地疼爱我，却仍旧沉默寡言。「吃过早饭，我就开始讲给你听。」他说，「你亲爱的母亲在哪儿？我怎么竟然……」

===Original===
Then of a sudden his memory returned, and he burst into tears.

I now saw, to my horror, that his mind was gone. When he recovered, he said: “It has all come back again, but at times now I am a blank, and every week am more and more so. I daresay I shall be sensible now for several hours. We will go into the study after breakfast, and I will talk to you as long as I can do so.”

Let the reader spare me, and let me spare the reader any description of what we both of us felt.

When we were in the study, my father said, “My dearest boy, get pen and paper and take notes of what I tell you. It will be all disjointed; one day I shall remember this, and another that, but there will not be many more days on which I shall remember anything at all. I cannot write a coherent page. You, when I am gone, can piece what I tell you together, and tell it as I should have told it if I had been still sound. But do not publish it yet; it might do harm to those dear good people. Take the notes now, and arrange them the sooner the better, for you may want to ask me questions, and I shall not be here much longer. Let publishing wait till you are confident that publication can do no harm; and above all, say nothing to betray the whereabouts of Erewhon, beyond admitting (which I fear I have already done) that it is in the Southern hemisphere.”

===Chinese===
猛然间，他全都回想起来，顿时泪如雨下。

我这才看清——心中的惊恐难以言喻——他的神志已经垮了。等他缓过神来，他说：「现在又都想起来了；可是如今我时不时便一片空白，而且一个星期比一个星期更甚。这会儿我大约还能清醒上几个钟头。吃过早饭我们就到书房去，我能讲多久，就同你讲多久。」

请读者饶过我，也让我饶过读者：我们父子二人当时的心情，就不必描摹了。

进了书房，父亲说：「我最亲爱的孩子，拿纸笔来，把我讲的都记下来。讲出来必定是零零碎碎：今天我想起这个，明天想起那个；可我还记得起任何事情的日子，已经没有几天了。我自己是写不出一页连贯的话的。等我去了以后，你可以把我讲的拼缀起来，照我神志还健全时本会讲的那样，把它讲出来。只是眼下先不要出版；那也许会妨害那些亲爱的好人。现在就记，越早整理越好，因为你往后也许还要问我，而我在世的日子不多了。出版的事，且等到你确信出版决无妨害时再说；最要紧的是，千万不可说破埃瑞洪的下落——至多承认它位于南半球（我怕这一层，我自己已经招认过了）。」

===Original===
These instructions I have religiously obeyed. For the first days after his return, my father had few attacks of loss of memory, and I was in hopes that his former health of mind would return when he found himself in his old surroundings. During these days he poured forth the story of his adventures so fast, that if I had not had a fancy for acquiring shorthand, I should not have been able to keep pace with him. I repeatedly urged him not to overtax his strength, but he was oppressed by the fear that if he did not speak at once, he might never be able to tell me all he had to say; I had, therefore, to submit, though seeing plainly enough that he was only hastening the complete paralysis which he so greatly feared.

Sometimes his narrative would be coherent for pages together, and he could answer any questions without hesitation; at others, he was now here and now there, and if I tried to keep him to the order of events he would say that he had forgotten intermediate incidents, but that they would probably come back to him, and I should perhaps be able to put them in their proper places.

After about ten days he seemed satisfied that I had got all the facts, and that with the help of the pamphlets which he had brought with him I should be able to make out a connected story. “Remember,” he said, “that I thought I was quite well so long as I was in Erewhon, and do not let me appear as anything else.”

When he had fully delivered himself, he seemed easier in his mind, but before a month had passed he became completely paralysed, and though he lingered till the beginning of June, he was seldom more than dimly conscious of what was going on around him.

His death robbed me of one who had been a very kind and upright elder brother rather than a father; and so strongly have I felt his influence still present, living and working, as I believe for better within me, that I did not hesitate to copy the epitaph which he saw in the Musical Bank at Fairmead,1 and to have it inscribed on the very simple monument which he desired should alone mark his grave.

===Chinese===
这些嘱咐，我一概恪遵不误。他回来后的最初几天，失忆的发作还不多；我一度指望，他回到旧日环境之后，昔日健全的神志会失而复得。那几天里，他的历险从他口中滔滔涌出；倘若不是我素来喜好学速记，简直跟不上他。我一再劝他不可过耗气力，但他被一种恐惧压着：怕不当场说出来，便再也不能把要说的都告诉我；因此我只好依他，尽管我看得清清楚楚，他这是在催促那场他深自畏惧的彻底瘫痪早一日到来。

有时他的叙述一连好几页头头是道，问什么都能毫不迟疑地作答；有时又忽而在这里、忽而在那里；我若想把他拉回事件的先后次序，他便说中间的情节已经忘了，不过多半还会想起来，到那时，我或许能把它们安放回原处。

约莫十天之后，他似乎放了心，认为事实我已记全，再借他随身带回的那些小册子之助，我应当能理出一个连贯的故事。「记住，」他说，「在埃瑞洪的时候，我自始至终自以为安然无恙；写的时候，也别叫我显得是别的样子。」

他把要说的话倾吐净尽之后，心里似乎松快了些；可是不出一个月，他便完全瘫痪了。虽说他还捱到 6 月初，却难得对周围发生的事，有哪怕蒙眬的知觉。

他的死，使我失去的与其说是一位父亲，不如说是一位仁厚正直的兄长；而他的影响至今仍在我身上存留、活着、起作用——我相信是叫我向好——其力之强，竟使我毫不犹豫地抄下他在费尔米德（Fairmead）音乐银行（Musical Bank）里见到的那段碑文¹，把它铭刻在他希望唯一用来标记自己坟墓的那座极简朴的纪念碑上。

---

===Original===
The foregoing was written in the summer of 1891; what I now add should be dated December 3, 1900. If, in the course of my work, I have misrepresented my father, as I fear I may have sometimes done, I would ask my readers to remember that no man can tell another’s story without some involuntary misrepresentation both of facts and characters. They will, of course, see that *Erewhon Revisited* is written by one who has far less literary skill than the author of *Erewhon*; but again I would ask indulgence on the score of youth, and the fact that this is my first book. It was written nearly ten years ago, i.e. in the months from March to August 1891, but for reasons already given it could not then be made public. I have now received permission, and therefore publish the following chapters, exactly, or very nearly exactly, as they were left when I had finished editing my father’s diaries, and the notes I took down from his own mouth﻿—with the exception, of course, of these last few lines, hurriedly written as I am on the point of leaving England, of the additions I made in 1892, on returning from my own three hours’ stay in Erewhon, and of the Postscript.

===Chinese===
上面这些文字写于 1891 年夏天；现在增补的一段，则当署 1900 年 12 月 3 日。倘若在叙述之中——我怕有时难免——我歪曲了父亲的形象，务请读者记住：无论何人转述他人的故事，都免不了在事实与性情两方面，有些不自觉的失真。读者当然看得出，《埃瑞洪重访》（*Erewhon Revisited*）出自一位文笔远逊于《埃瑞洪》（*Erewhon*）作者之手；但我仍要再度请求宽宥：一来因为年轻，二来因为这毕竟是我的第一本书。它写于将近十年前，即 1891 年 3 月至 8 月那几个月，只因前面说过的缘故，当时未能公之于世。如今我已获得许可，故将以下各章公之于世——除了此刻我行将离英、匆促写下的最后这几行，1892 年我自己在埃瑞洪逗留三个钟头归来后所作的增补，以及书末的后记之外——都照我当年编定父亲日记与我亲手记下的口述笔记时所留下的样子，或极相近的样子，刊布出来。

## XVIII Yram Invites Dr. Downie and Mrs. Humdrum to Luncheon﻿—A Passage at Arms Between Her and Hanky Is Amicably Arranged / 十八、雅拉姆邀唐尼博士与亨德伦太太共进午宴——她与汉基的一场交锋以和气收场

===Original===
The disturbance caused by my father’s outbreak was quickly suppressed, for George got him out of the temple almost immediately; it was bruited about, however, that the Sunchild had come down from the palace of the sun, but had disappeared as soon as anyone had tried to touch him. In vain did Hanky try to put fresh life into his sermon; its back had been broken, and large numbers left the church to see what they could hear outside, or failing information, to discourse more freely with one another.
Hanky did his best to quiet his hearers when he found that he could not infuriate them﻿—
“This poor man,” he said, “is already known to me, as one of those who have deluded themselves into believing that they are the Sunchild. I have known of his so declaring himself, more than once, in the neighbourhood of Bridgeford, and others have not infrequently done the same; I did not at first recognize him, and regret that the shock of horror his words occasioned me should have prompted me to suggest violence against him. Let this unfortunate affair pass from your minds, and let me again urge upon you the claims of the Sunchild Evidence Society.”
The audience on hearing that they were to be told more about the Sunchild Evidence Society melted away even more rapidly than before, and the sermon fizzled out to an ignominious end quite unworthy of its occasion.
===Chinese===
我父亲那一场发作掀起的骚乱很快便平息下去，因为乔治几乎当场就把他带出了神殿；然而风声照样传了开去，说太阳之子确曾从太阳的宫殿里降了下来，只是谁一伸手要碰他，他便立刻不见了。汉基竭力想给自己的布道重新续上一口气，终归徒劳——那篇布道的脊梁骨已经断了。大批会众离席而去，到外面看看能听到些什么；打听不着，便索性彼此放开来高谈阔论。
汉基眼见煽不起会众的怒火，便转而尽力安抚他们——
「这个可怜人，」他说，「我早有耳闻，他是那班自欺欺人、当真相信自己就是太阳之子的人们中间的一个。我知道他不止一次在布里奇福德左近如此自称，行此道的旁人也不算少；起初我没有认出他来，他的话叫我惊骇失措，竟至提议对他动武，这是我很引为憾恨的。请把这桩不幸的事情从你们心里搁开吧；也请容我再次吁请诸位，支持太阳之子证据会的事业。」
听众一听说接下来还有太阳之子证据会的详情可听，便散得比先前更快；那篇布道于是噗地一声泄了气，落得个极不光彩的收场，与这场合的体面全然不相称。

===Original===
About half-past twelve, the service ended, and Hanky went to the robing-room to take off his vestments. Yram, the Mayor, and Panky, waited for him at the door opposite to that through which my father had been taken; while waiting, Yram scribbled off two notes in pencil, one to Dr. Downie, and another to Mrs. Humdrum, begging them to come to lunch at once﻿—for it would be one o’clock before they could reach the Mayor’s. She gave these notes to the Mayor, and bade him bring both the invited guests along with him.
The Mayor left just as Hanky was coming towards her. “This, Mayoress,” he said with some asperity, “is a very serious business. It has ruined my collection.
